This document describes problems and discrepancies in observations on
this data volume.

Information for Scientific Users of HiRISE Color Products By Alfred
McEwen and Eric Eliason, October 10, 2007
===================================================================

HiRISE PDS releases consist of a set of official products, and a set of
12 "Extra" products for each image.  Below are examples for hypothetical
HiRISE image PSP_005000_1000.

Official PDS products:

Experiment Data Records (EDRs) -- This is the raw data, usually in 28
channels for each image.  Only users with specialized needs (such as
producing topographic models from stereo pairs) will need to access
these images.   In addition, if there are concerns about whether or not
an unusual color marking is an artifact, it is best to examine the EDRs.
The color data is constructed from 6 CCDs out of 14 total: RED4, RED5,
BG12, BG13, IR10, IR11.

Reduced Data Records (RDRs) -- These are radiometrically calibrated,
geometrically mapped images with lossless JPEG2000 compression (file
names end in .JP2).   Lossless compression means that the data volume is
reduced without sacrificing any information.  The full HiRISE RDR
information is contained in just two products:

PSP_005000_1000_RED.JP2 Grayscale mosaic of all RED-filter channels.
PSP_005000_1000_COLOR.JP2 IR, RED, BG 3-color product over center 20%
of the RED swath width. Additionally, each JP2 image also has a PDS
label (companion .LBL file) providing useful information about the
observation such as map projection and viewing information.

RDR Extras:

The official PDS products are enormous files, typically larger than 1 GB
in size, and quite difficult to work with.  So we also create a set of
RDR "Extras" at reduced scale or with slightly "lossy" compression to
produce smaller files that are much easier to display.  "Lossy"
compression eliminates some small-scale detail (such as random noise),
which enables compression into smaller files that are easier to
transfer, store, and manipulate.

First, there are 2 basic types and scales of Extra RDR products: those
ending in .JP2 are JPEG2000 images at the full spatial scale. those
ending in .jpg are JPEG products at reduced scale--called browse images.

Next, there are 2 geometries: Those that include NOMAP in the file name
are not map-projected, but the up to 10 RED CCD images or 2 sets of
color CCD images are stitched together to show all of the data. Those
without NOMAP in the file name are map projected.

There are 6 map-projected Extra products: 
PSP_005000_1000_RED.QLOOK.JP2 same as official PDS RED (or grayscale)
product except with lossy compression to reduce the file size.
PSP_005000_1000_RED.browse.jpg browse version (2048 pixels wide)
PSP_005000_1000_RED.abrowse.jpg browse version with title and scale bar
PSP_005000_1000_COLOR.QLOOK.JP2 same as official PDS color product 
except with a contrast stretch applied to each color band and lossy
compression. 
PSP_005000_1000_COLOR.browse.jpg browse version (2048 pixels wide)
PSP_005000_1000_COLOR.abrowse.jpg browse version with labels and scale
bar

There are 6 NOMAP products types:
PSP_005000_1000_RED.NOMAP.JP2 up to 10 RED-filter CCD images stitched 
together
PSP_05000_1000_RED.NOMAP.browse.jpg  browse version
PSP_005000_1000_IRB.NOMAP.JP2  3-color image consisting of IR, RED, and
BG images.  The IR and BG images have been warped to line up with the
RED.NOMAP image
PSP_05000_1000_IRB.NOMAP.browse.jpg  browse version
PSP_005000_1000_RGB.NOMAP.JP2  3-color image consisting of RED, BG, and
synthetic blue images.  The BG image has been warped to line up with the
RED.NOMAP image.  The BG (blue-green) bandpass primarily accepts green
light.  The synthetic blue image digital numbers (DNs) consist of the BG
image DN multiplied by 2 minus 30% of the RED image DN for each pixel.
This is not unique data, but provides a more appealing way to display
the color variations present in just two bandpasses, RED and BG.
PSP_005000_1000_RGB.NOMAP.browse.jpg  browse version

Note that the NOMAP products are in the geometry of our raw images.
Since MRO flies north mostly on the dayside of Mars, most of these
images have north down (actually ~7 degrees tilted from straight down),
except near the North and South Poles where images have many possible
orientations.

What do the colors in HiRISE images mean?

The PDS products are stretched differently from the products in the
Extras directory.  The PDS-product color is given the same minimum and
maximum value, in calibrated units of reflectance (I/F), for each color
bandpass.  The result is that the colors are relatively bland, but they
are also consistent from place to place on Mars.  (The PDS label (.LBL
file) provides the information for converting the DN value to I/F: I/F =
DN*SCALING_FACTOR + OFFSET.)  It isn't natural color, as seen by normal
human eyes, because the IR, RED, and BG channels are displayed in red,
green, and blue colors.   For the Extras products, each color band is
individually stretched to maximize contrast, so the colors are enhanced
differently for each image based on the color and brightness of each
scene.  Scenes with dark shadows and bright sunlit slopes or with both
bright and dark materials are stretched less, so the colors are less
enhanced than is the case over bland scenes.

In spite of the variable level of color enhancement for the Extras
products, we can make some generalizations to better understand what the
stretched color images are showing.   Dust (or indurated dust) is
generally the reddest material present and looks reddish in the RGB
color and yellow in the IRB color.  Coarser-grained materials (sand and
rocks) are generally bluer (or sometimes purplish in IRB color) but also
relatively dark, except where coated by dust.  Frost and ice are also
relatively blue, but bright, and often concentrated at the poles or on
pole-facing slopes.   Some bedrock is also relatively bright and blue,
but not as much as frost or ice, and it has distinctive morphologies.
The IR and RED bandpasses are often highly correlated so the IR provides
little new information, and the RGB color does a better job of showing
the RED vs. blue-green color variations by moving these differences into
the warmer colors.   Also, one of the four channels of IR data has
special problems and may be excluded from the RDR products or have
anomalies, whereas the RGB color always covers the full color swath,
unless some data was lost in transmission to Earth.   The IR channels do
provide unique information in some small but important areas on Mars,
particularly when the mineral olivine or a diversity of minerals altered
by water are present.   The best way to understand what the colors
indicate about composition is to compare them to mineral maps derived
from the 18 m/pixel CRISM data (see http://crism.jhuapl.edu/).  HiRISE
color images enable extrapolation of the CRISM results to smaller
spatial scales.

Another benefit of the color is that it helps resolve ambiguities in
grayscale images.  One region may be brighter or darker than another due
to different materials on the surface or due to different slopes and
angles of illumination, and it can be difficult to make this distinction
by examining a grayscale image.  With the color images this ambiguity is
resolved because different materials have different colors.   Stereo
images provide an alternate means of resolving this ambiguity.

Radiometric calibration of the data into units of I/F (ratio of
reflected intensity to incident intensity of sunlight in each color
bandpass) is a work in progress.  It is probably accurate to only about
the 10% level, but we hope to improve this in the future.   Atmospheric
variability introduces a comparable or even larger uncertainty in the
I/F of surface materials.

Artifacts to watch out for

The colors are not always perfectly coregistered.  If you see parallel
bands of intense colors near bright-dark edges, that is probably
misregistration.  But the actual color of Mars can give this appearance
as well, but with more muted colors, due to aeolian processes.

Another apparent artifact is the variable color of shadows.  Shadows
really do have different colors (as on Earth), but can get exaggerated
by the automatic contrast stretches (lowest 0.1% of values are set to
black and highest 0.01% are set to white, with linear scaling between
these values; this is done independently in each color).

This automatic contrast stretching can also create unusual colors
(actually more realistic colors) in non frost/ice materials when bright
frost or ice is present, as these bright patches control the high values
set to white.

Sometimes there are erroneous colors near the center of each set of
CCDs, or near samples 1000 and 3000 in unbinned NOMAP images (samples
500 and 1500 in 2x2 binned images).   This is due to special calibration
problems in the center columns, where the data is split and read out
into the two channels.

Some of the early HiRISE images include saturation due to the choice of
Look-Up Table (LUT) for onboard conversion of the data from 14 to 8 bits
per pixel.  If the very brightest highlights have strange colors, this
could be due to saturation.

Sometimes small spots, lines, or jagged patterns will be unusually
bright in one of the colors.  These are probably cosmic-ray hits.

Rectangular patterns with very bright colors are where data is missing
from one or more color channels due to loss of data telemetry from MRO
to the ground stations.

Images obscured by atmospheric dust or haze generally appear noisy.  The
original images are not noisy but they are bland, so the stretches
applied to best see the surface features also enhance the noise.
Condensate hazes can also create diffuse color variability that does not
correspond to the distribution of surface materials, especially in the
BG.

Two of the CCDs have special noise or DN dropouts due to poor
performance of an electronic part when cold: CCDs IR10 and RED9.  These
bad data sometimes lead to poor contrast stretches and odd colors.  One
other CCD, BG13, has on very rare occasions (twice that we know of while
imaging Mars) suffered from a malfunction that creates down-column
smear.   Due to these problems we have sometimes applied more pixel
binning (4x4 rather than 2x2) to BG13 or IR10 than to BG12 or IR11,
resulting in loss of color resolution over half of the color image.  We
process the images in a way that adds the RED-image detail to the IR and
BG images, which are usually binned to a greater degree than RED4 and
RED5, so the images have the best possible grayscale resolution in spite
of lower color resolution.


31 March 2007 Focal Plane System Degradation
============================================
Soon after the start of the orbital science operations, 
several HiRISE detectors experienced increased noise levels 
impacting the quality of returned imaging. These problems 
have largely been mitigated by changes in how the HiRISE 
instrument is operated.  Starting on March 31, 2007 the 
HiRISE instrument commanding sequence was modified to allow 
for a 200-second warmup of instrument electronics before 
acquiring an image. When taking this action, instrument 
noise returned to nominal levels for most observations.

The following is a summary of image noise problems prior to 
March 31, 2007:

  -  IR10 Channel 1 (IR10/1) imaging rarely produced useful 
data from the start of orbital science observations to 
March 31st, 2007. Some IR10/1 imaging is good when a 
preceding science observation was in close proximity in time 
to a previous observation causing the electronics to have 
elevated temperatures.

  -  IR10/0 imaging had elevated noise levels but provided 
useful imaging.

  -  RED9/1 started rapid degradation in mid-November 2006. 
Many observations were poor quality but were still used in 
the production of RDR products whenever possible.

  -  RED9/0 experienced minor degradation in December 2006 but 
continued to produce useful imaging.

  -  RED0/1, RED1/1, RED3/1, RED8/0, RED8/1 experienced 
degraded image quality aquired during the last week of January 
2007 when the instrument temperatures were intentionally 
lowered to conduct instrument performance testing. 


24 August 2007 Focal Plane System Degradation Update
====================================================
Diagnostic tests and months of stable, successful operation have 
resolved concerns raised early this year about long-term 
prospects for the powerful telescopic camera on NASA's Mars 
Reconnaissance Orbiter.

The High Resolution Imaging Science Experiment (HiRISE) 
camera on the orbiter has now taken more than 3,000 images of 
Mars, resolving features as small as a desk in targeted areas 
covering thousands of square miles of the Martian surface. 
Already, this is the largest Mars data set ever acquired by a 
single experiment. The camera is one of six instruments on the 
orbiter.

During the first three months after the orbiter's primary 
science phase began in November, researchers saw an increase in 
noise and pixel dropouts in data from seven of the camera's 14 
detectors. The effects on image quality were small in all but 
two detectors, but the trend raised concerns noted in a Feb. 7 
news release online at 
http://www.jpl.nasa.gov/news/news.cfm?release=2007-013.

Tests have yielded an explanation for the earlier pattern, and 
the camera's performance record shows the noise stopped getting 
worse after about three to four months of the science phase.

Alfred McEwen of the University of Arizona, Tucson, principal 
investigator for the camera, said, "I'm happy to report that 
there has been no detectable degradation over the past five 
months."

A team at Ball Aerospace & Technologies Corp., Boulder, Colo., 
designer and builder of the instrument, has used an engineering 
model of the camera's focal-plane system to successfully duplicate 
the problem. This has helped in understanding causes and in testing 
a procedure for warming the focal-plane electronics prior to each 
image. One cause is that an electrical interface lacked extra 
capability beyond minimum requirements. Another cause is an 
unexpected change in performance of another electronic component 
over the course of the first thousand or so large images. With 
pre-warming, the camera acquires good data from all detectors, 
though minor noise remains an issue in data from one of two channels 
of one detector collecting infrared imagery.

McEwen said, "Given the stability we've seen and understanding the 
nature of the problem, we now expect HiRISE to return high-quality 
data for years to come."

The Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter mission is managed by NASA's Jet 
Propulsion Laboratory, Pasadena, Calif., for NASA's Science Mission 
Directorate, Washington. Lockheed Martin Space Systems, Denver, 
Colo., is the prime contractor and built the spacecraft.


25 August 2007 Problems in the Computation of I/F for Radiometric
Correction
====================================================================
An error was detected in the computation of the Mars-Sun distance
when calculating the I/F normalization resulting in I/F errors as
large as 7%. This problem is seen in the red-filter RDR products for
both the June/07 and September/07 release to the Planetary Data 
System.  An update to the I/F normalization will be included in 
a future reprocessing effort for the RDR products.


10 April 2008 GeoTiff boxes added to RDRs
=========================================

On 10 April 2008 the HiRISE team began producing RDR products that
have GeoTiff "boxes" imbedded into the RDR JPEG2000 image files.  
This feature enabled many GIS and image processing packages  
to better access and use the map projection information. 
The new HiRISE RDR products can be identified by the DATA_SET_ID
and DATA_SET_NAME keywords found in the PDS labels:
MRO-M-HIRSE-3-RDR-V1.1. Earlier RDR were reprocessed with this
update.


09 June 2009 Gain Matrices Updated
==================================
Calibration gain matrices were updated prior to resuming observation
processing on 09 June 2009. Observation ESP_013452_2155 and later
observations made use of these updates immediately.  All products
in which the PRODUCT_CREATION_TIME label parameter is greater than
or equal to 09 June 2009 will make use of the updated gain coefficients.
Observations acquired prior to 09 June 2009 have been reprocessed to
take advantage of these updates. Reprocessing was completed in 2010.
The change to I/F values could be as much as 10%. 


05 July 2012 DTM Updates
========================

1. The naming convention of orthorectified images has changed to 
   more closely match the standard product id's of the source RDRs.
   The DTM naming convention will remain unchanged.
2. In addition, orthorectified imagery from repeat observations
   covering the same area as a DTM will be added to the PDS volume.
   These new products will be located in the same directory as the
   DTM used for orthorectification.
3. Finally, color orthoimagery will start being added as well.


05 July 2012 INDEX Table Updates
================================

A HICAL_VERSION field has been added to the EDR index tables.
This field indicates the ISIS software build version, the date
of the ISIS release, and the date of the last updates made to the
hical program that was used to calibrate the EDR that was used to
create the final RDR product.

A SOURCE_DTM_ID column has been added to the DTM index tables.
This field (in the case of orthoimages) indicates the DTM that
what used to orthorectify the image. 


08 December 2014 Information About Siding Spring Images
=======================================================

The CSS images were taken under off-nominal conditions and require
special processing. These include all observations taken during orbits
38,549 to 38,589.

1. In general the IR10 files are bad and do noy contain the comet.
2. Most observations were taken at exposure times of 2.56 seconds and
   contain very high levels of non-uniform dark current.
3. The comet is at or below the noise threshold for most of the
   observations.
4. A set of processed files may be available in the future.


04 February 2015 Improved Observations
======================================

Data were recovered for the following observations, filling
in gaps. The images were reprocessed and new versions made
available on the PDS.

ESP_027180_1880 - completed observation released 04 February 2015


04 May 2015 CK Kernels Updated
==============================

MRO weekly constructed CK production run by the NAIF between September
2014 and March 2015 used an out of data SCLK kernel. These kernels were
regenerated by the NAIF and delivered in April 2015. HiRISE testing of
the new CK kernels found minor differences in latitude and longitude,
between 10 and 100 pixels, in the corners and center of HiRISE images
and the decision was made not to reprocess these images at this
time,but a future reprocessing will use the updated kernels.


DSMAP.CAT Update
================

The DSMAP.CAT file was updated to correct equations for converting
sample and lines to latitude and longitude by adding previously
omitted conversions between radians and degrees. An errant negative
sign was removed from the conversion from y map coordinates to image
array coordinates.


15 June 2015 DSMAP.CAT Update
=============================

The DSMAP.CAT file was updated with the current data set ID
MRO-M-HIRISE-3-RDR-V1.1

Science Data Not Returned
=========================
The following EDR products were planned but not created 
because the raw science data for the products were never 
received at the HiRISE Operations Center (HiROC). A likely 
cause is the failure of the Deep Space Network to receive 
telemetry data from the Mars Reconnaissance Orbiter at the 
time science data for the product were being transmitted to 
Earth. Some of these data may be included in problematic
raw science data, including those described below in the
"EDR Products Could Not Be Processed" section. These may be
recovered at a later time as HiRISE data restoration tools
improve.

losses that prevented the EDR from being properly 
constructed. EDR products may be recovered at a later time 
as HiRISE data restoration tools improve.

Calibration Problems
====================
The following EDR products failed to go through the 
calibration step. This occurs when images have experienced 
high instrument instability problems.  The metadata 
internal to the science observation could not properly 
characterize the calibration correction. In these cases, 
the EDR Product is included as part of the HiRISE EDR 
archive but is not included in the generation of the RDR 
product. These products may be included in future RDR 
versions as calibration techniques improve.

PSP_001345_2480_RED4_1.IMG

BIN 8 IR and BG Channels
========================
Channels commanded with BIN 8 cannot currently be processed through
calibration. In these cases, the EDR Product is included as part of
the HiRISE EDR archive but is not included in the generation of the
RDR product.

Improper HiRISE Commanding
==========================
The following Observations had instrument commanding 
improperly chosen for the observing conditions. The result 
was under or over-saturated images that provide little or 
no useful scientific information. These products are 
included in the HiRISE EDR archive but are generally not included in 
the generation of RDR products.  Occasionally a RED RDR can be
generated, and will be flagged below with RED RDR included.

PSP_001592_2530 - RED RDR included
PSP_001710_2785 - RED RDR included
PSP_001996_1240
PSP_002021_1850
PSP_002367_2515


IR10 Warm up Images
===================
A few observations contain only IR10 EDR products. These 
observations were conducted as an experiment to determine 
if IR10 image quality could be improved by warming up the 
electronics before acquiring a science observation. These products are 
included in the EDR archive but are not included in the generation of 
RDR products

PSP_001620_1465
PSP_001641_1515

Color Issues Due to Cosmic Ray Hits
===================================
The following color and geometry products have bad
false color or faint products due to bad stretches
caused by cosmic ray hits or other particularly
bright spots.

PSP_003084_2085
ESP_048684_1530
ESP_049792_1885
ESP_050156_1205

The following images included cosmic ray hits. These
saturated pixels have been nulled to improve the color
stretch and false color products.

ESP_049493_1350


Geometry Products Not Produced
==============================
The following geometry products could not be produced
because of processing issues that may be fixed with
future software updates.

PSP_004471_2030 - Version 2
PSP_006913_1765 - Version 2
PSP_007134_1435 - Version 2
PSP_007519_2170 - Version 2


Geometry Products Using Predicted SPICE
=======================================
The following geometry products could not be produced
using the reconstructed CK SPICE kernel. They were
instead produced using the predicted CK SPICE kernel.

PSP_007960_1470
(used mro_sc_psp_080331_080412p.bc)
ESP_011398_1275
(used mro_sc_psp_081229_090110p.bc)
ESP_019158_2665
(used CK_Pred_10236_10248_sc_20100824162507.bc)
ESP_029446_1885
(used CK_Pred_12311_12323_sc_20121106175104.bc)
ESP_034472_1960
(used CK_Pred_13337_13349_sc_20131203194105.bc)


Corrupted EDRs
==============
The first few lines of image data in EDRs from the 
following 745 observations were inadvertently
corrupted by software used to update the descriptions
in their labels. All EDRs with a creation date later 
than November 4, 2009 will not be impacted by the corruption
problem due to a fix applied to our production software.
Anyone who has downloaded HiRISE EDRs prior to the 
December 2009 release should check the list below to 
ensure any EDRs being worked with are not on this list.

28 June 2010 DTM Orthoimage labels correction
=============================================

An error in the generation of the SCALING_FACTOR and 
OFFSET values in the orthoimage labels prior to this 
date caused these two values to be swapped. This error 
does not affect the image data. The only effect of this 
error is in the conversion of the pixel Digital Number (DN) 
values to Intensity/Flux (I/F) values. The corrected 
SCALING_FACTOR and OFFSET values now result in the correct 
conversion from DN to I/F following the formula in the label.
The .LBL files for the following products may have had 
incorrect values for these keywords if they were downloaded 
prior to 6/28/2010.

PSP_001414_1780_PSP_001612_1780
PSP_001432_2015_PSP_001630_2015
PSP_001472_2785_PSP_001710_2785

9 November 2015 Planetary radius incorrect in some
orthoimages
==================================================

Following the standard map projection definition for
HiRISE PDS products in the Equirectangular projection
(-65 to +65 degrees latitude), a customized planetary
radius is calculated at 5 degree latitude bins. The
images are then map projected onto a sphere of that radius,
with the latitude of true scale being the binned latitude
(next lowest 5 degrees from the center latitude of the image).
This minimizes distortion using the Equirectangular projection
at higher latitudes.

In HiRISE PDS image products--JPEG2000 format (.JP2) with
detached PDS text label (.LBL)--geospatial headers are embedded
within the .JP2 files. Mapping definitions are also recorded in
the detached PDS labels.

Orthoimages are special image products created by controlling
HiRISE images to a given stereo pair, and then orthorectifying
them to the Digital Terrain Model (DTM) made from that stereo
pair. A DTM will have at least orthoimages made from the stereo
pair, and possibly additional orthoimages acquired over the
same target. Additional orthoimages are typically made to study
surface and topographic changes.

Due to a programming error, the planetary radius (semi-major
axis) reported in the geotiff header in the additional
orthoimages is incorrect. Instead of encoding the customized
planetary radius for the binned latitude, the equatorial radius
of Mars is recorded. The problem affects the geolocation of the
orthoimages relative to the DTM and the stereo pair orthoimages
when using geospatial software to analyze and compare these
products, if the software is using the geoJP2 header to display
the data. The planetary radius is reported correctly in the
detached PDS label, as the value for the keywords A_AXIS_RADIUS,
B_AXIS_RADIUS, and C_AXIS_RADIUS. Users may see an offset in the
latitude direction when comparing additional orthoimages for a
given DTM to the orthoimages derived from the stereo pair used
to generate the DTM. This offset will be more noticeable at
higher latitudes.

Products affected:
- Additional orthoimages (in addition to the stereo pair used
to create the DTM).
- Products in the Equirectangular map projection.
- Polar Stereographic products are not affected.
- Additional orthoimages with CENTER_LATITUDE between 5 and
65 degrees north and south.
- Orthoimages with a CENTER_LATITUDE of zero (0) degrees are not
affected.

A correction to this issue will be implemented for existing
products in the HiRISE DTM catalogue, as well as future products
to be released to the PDS.


15 February 2016 Unfiltered COLOR RDRs
===========================================================

Observations acquired after orbit 41800 or any RDRs with
product creation times after July 2, 2015 now include
COLOR RDRs with no cosmetic noise filtering applied,
improved color stretches, and improved color registration.

Progress on the release of reprocessed COLOR RDRs will be
in future PDS data release announcements.


4 May 2016 Updated Version of DTEEC_010277_1650_010699_1650
DTM Project
===========================================================

Updated version of DTEEC_010277_1650_010699_1650 DTM project
processed. Check the SOURCE_PRODUCT_ID parameter in the
orthoimage label to see which version of the DTM the
orthoimage is derived from.


1 June 2019 Updated Version of DTEEC_024954_1720_025020_1720_A01
DTM Project
===========================================================

Sequence 1 orthoimages from DTEEC_024954_1720_025020_1720_A01
project replaced with sequence 2 orthoimages to fix a
geometric offset between orthoimages and DTMs.


06 October 2020 ADC Settings Update
===========================================================

Data acquired after Saturday, August 01, 2020 and starting
with observation ID ESP_065703_0865 have different ADC
(Analog-to Digital Converter) settings and the accuracy of
the calibration has not yet been fully vetted for these
new settings. 


15 February 2021 Updated Coordinates for
DTEEC_035828_2255_035327_2255_A01 
===========================================================

The center and corner coordinates have been corrected for
products associated with DTEEC_035828_2255_035327_2255_A01.

===========================================================

These geometry products were processed in 2011 after an
update to ISIS but were not released to the PDS until now.

ESP_016859_2420
ESP_016988_1495


31 August 2023 CCD RED9 Status Update
===========================================================

The electronics unit for CCD RED9 failed in August 2011,
reducing the RED filter coverage in width at one end of
the images.


31 August 2023 CCD RED4 Status Update
===========================================================

The electronics unit for CCD RED4 started to fail in August
2023, and we have not been acquiring image data in this
central swath of the images. The processing pipelines will be
updated to fill this gap with the IR10 image for some products.
The 3-color coverage is now reduced in width.


09 January 2024 Release 98A Notes
===========================================================

Release 98A (January 2024) is a DTM-only release, as no new
data was acquired during Mars solar conjunction.


06 February 2024 CCD RED4 Status Update
===========================================================
We continued to command CPMM5 (CCD RED4) after its initial
failures in August 2023 until it began failing for most
observations in January 2024. When CPMM5 was commanded but
failed, the resulting raw data file included headers but no
image data were returned for RED4_0 and RED4_1. In the same
file, the header and image data for the next channel, IR10_0
(CPMM6_0) were included. We expect to be able to recover
these missing IR10_0 products from the RED4_0 raw data files
in the near future. We will reprocess these observations and
update ERRATA.TXT as necessary.


04 February 2025 Geometry Offsets Status
===========================================================
Geometry offsets of up to 100 meters have been measured in
some observations reprocessed with more recent versions of
the USGS Integrated Software for Imagers and Spectrometers.
The more recent versions of these observations were created
with updated SPICE kernels as well. Observations through
orbit range 20599 have been reprocessed, and observations
from orbit range 41800 have the newest processing.
Observations between orbit ranges 20600 and 41799 have not
yet been reprocessed.


28 February 2025 CCD RED4 Status Update
===========================================================
With an update to operate the HiRISE camera at higher
temperatures on 23 November 2024, the success rate of CPMM5
(CCD RED4) allowed us to command it again for a short time
beginning 11 January 2025. During this time, when CPMM5
failed for an observation, we recovered IR10_0 and completed
image processing with the procedures noted in the 06 February
2024 CCD RED4 Status Update. The success rate dropped quickly
in late February 2025, however, and we will stop commanding
CPMM5 on 8 March 2025. We will return to filling the RED4
gap with IR10 data as noted in the 31 August 2023 CCD RED4
Status and the 06 February 2024 CCD RED4 Status Updates.


01 July 2025 CCD RED4 Status Update
===========================================================
We have resumed commanding the RED4 CCD as of 28 June 2025.
While we expect RED4 to fail for some observations, we expect
a high percentage of successes for other observations. The
EDRgen processing pipeline has been updated to automatically
recover IR10_0 from the raw data file that results when RED4
fails. IR10_0 and IR10_1 are used to fill in the RED4 gap in
RED NOMAP and RGB NOMAP products currently, and in the RED
portion of the MIRB and MRGB merged products. These channels
will be used to fill in the RED4 filter gap in the color
portion of the MRGB product in the future. The RED4 gap is
not filled in IRB NOMAP products, as this does not make sense
for a product that already has a synthetic IR layer in it.
The RED4 gap is not filled in RED or COLOR RDR geometry
products either, because of RDR product requirements.


28 August 2025 ISIS Upgraded to Version 8.0.1
===========================================================
We upgraded the Integrated Software for Imagers and
Spectrometers (ISIS) we use for processing HiRISE observations
from version 6.0.0 to 8.0.1. In observations in orbit ranges
89000 and 89100, both versions were used to create calibration,
color, and geometry products, while later observations were
processed with version 8.0.1 only. Geometry products for
observation ESP_089087_1710 and greater were processed with
version 8.0.1.


06 May 2026 HiMosMerge Issues
===========================================================
Issues with the HiMosMerge processing pipeline prevents
merged MIRB and MRGB products from being created for a small
number of observations. ESP_092158_2360, ESP_092407_2350,
ESP_092504_2365 and additional products for ESP_089085_2565
and ESP_091769_1330 will be released in the future when the
processing issues have been resolved.


30 June 2026 HiMosMerge Issues Resolved
===========================================================
Merged MIRB and MRGB have been successfully created and
released for ESP_091769_1330 and ESP_092407_2350.

04 August 2026 Reprocessing
===========================================================
HiRISE observations are being reprocessed to include general
improvements made to the processing pipelines since the
beginning of the mission and to convert image product labels
for the Planetary Data System version 4 (PDS4.) 

Some image products could not be fully reprocessed due to
failures in the modern processing pipelines. In those cases,
old intermediate products have been used to reprocess new
versions of the image products with PDS4 labels.

04 August 2026 Recovered Channels
===========================================================
The following channels were recovered and included in their
respective observations' image products during the 2026
reprocessing of HiRISE observations:

PSP_001345_2480_RED4_1.IMG
PSP_001794_2190_RED7_0.IMG
